People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Like I can’t quite settle in my own body, especially if I’m supposed to sit still and deal with paperwork or household plans. When I’m home especially, I’ll start laundry, then suddenly I’m checking emails, then I’m reorganizing a drawer or looking at the calendar. It’s not really planned. I just kind of keep moving from one thing to the next.\n\nDoctor: Are these impulsive actions causing problems for you or those around you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I don’t really like saying that, but people at home and in the volunteer stuff I do can’t always count on me. I’ll mean to follow through, and then things just slip or get half-done. I’ve had to step back from some responsibilities because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty completing tasks because you lose interest or because you get distracted by other things?\n\nPatient: Mostly I get distracted, I think. Like if I’m at the kitchen table trying to finish something, my phone buzzes or I hear a car outside, or I suddenly think about dinner… and then I’m just off doing something else before I even realize I stopped.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this restlessness and difficulty focusing at work or in other settings outside of your home as well?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not really work, since I’m not working right now. But outside home, yes, in volunteer things. People there… and at home too… haven’t always been able to count on me, because I’ll mean to do something and then it falls through. I’ve had to step back from some responsibilities because of that.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this restlessness and difficulty focusing in social situations or when you're alone?\n\nPatient: More when I’m alone, I think… or when I’m supposed to just sit quietly and deal with paperwork or household plans. I get this feeling like I can’t really settle in my own body. In social situations, I can usually hide it a bit better.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restlessness and difficulty focusing are affecting your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. People at home, and with volunteering, they can’t always count on me the way they should. I try, but things still fall through, and I’ve had to step back from some responsibilities because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these feelings of restlessness and difficulty focusing at any time of the day, or are they worse at specific times?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure there’s a clear time of day. I haven’t really tracked it like that. It just sort of depends on what I’m trying to do, I guess.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

Like I can’t quite settle in my own body, especially if I’m supposed to sit still and deal with paperwork or household plans. I haven’t really tracked it like that. It’s been noticeably worse since I got married and our routines kind of merged. I’ll be trying to do something at the kitchen table, and then my phone buzzes or a car goes by, or I suddenly think about dinner, and I’m just… off somewhere else before I even realize I stopped.\n\nDoctor: Is the restlessness you feel more like a physical urge to move or a feeling in your mind?\n\nPatient: More physical, I think. Like I can’t quite settle in my own body, especially if I’m supposed to sit still and deal with paperwork or household plans. I just get… uneasy.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulses when you're feeling restless, like getting up and doing something else without thinking it through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. When I’m home especially, I’ll start laundry, then suddenly I’m checking emails, then I’m reorganizing a drawer or looking at the calendar. It’s not really planned. I just kind of keep moving from one thing to the next.\n\nDoctor: Are these impulsive actions causing problems for you or those around you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I don’t really like saying that, but people at home and in the volunteer stuff I do can’t always count on me. I’ll mean to follow through, and then things just slip or get half-done. I’ve had to step back from some responsibilities because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty completing tasks because you lose interest or because you get distracted by other things?\n\nPatient: Mostly I get distracted, I think. Like if I’m at the kitchen table trying to finish something, my phone buzzes or I hear a car outside, or I suddenly think about dinner… and then I’m just off doing something else before I even realize I stopped.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this restlessness and difficulty focusing at work or in other settings outside of your home as well?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not really work, since I’m not working right now. But outside home, yes, in volunteer things. People there… and at home too… haven’t always been able to count on me, because I’ll mean to do something and then it falls through. I’ve had to step back from some responsibilities because of that.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this restlessness and difficulty focusing in social situations or when you're alone?\n\nPatient: More when I’m alone, I think… or when I’m supposed to just sit quietly and deal with paperwork or household plans. I get this feeling like I can’t really settle in my own body. In social situations, I can usually hide it a bit better.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restlessness and difficulty focusing are affecting your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. People at home, and with volunteering, they can’t always count on me the way they should. I try, but things still fall through, and I’ve had to step back from some responsibilities because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these feelings of restlessness and difficulty focusing at any time of the day, or are they worse at specific times?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure there’s a clear time of day. I haven’t really tracked it like that. It just sort of depends on what I’m trying to do, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant problems with organization or time management, such as keeping track of tasks or appointments?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I lose track of things more than I’d like. Like I’ll mean to return a call, pay a bill, or bring something with me, and then suddenly days have passed, or I’m already in the car when I remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods where your restlessness and difficulty focusing were so severe that they significantly interfered with your daily life for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s been noticeably worse since I got married and our routines kind of merged. That was about… a year and five months ago.\n\nDoctor: How old were you when you first started noticing these symptoms of restlessness and difficulty focusing?\n\nPatient: Probably elementary school. I don’t remember an exact age, but looking back… it was kind of already there. I had the messy desk, forgot permission slips, and teachers would write notes that I talked out of turn or couldn’t stay seated.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of restlessness and difficulty focusing been present for at least six months before you got married?\n\nPatient: Yes. It was there way before I got married… like, back in elementary school. I was the kid with the messy desk, forgetting permission slips, and teachers writing that I talked too much or couldn’t stay seated.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

first started noticing these symptoms of restlessness and difficulty focusing?\n\nPatient: Probably elementary school. I don’t remember an exact age, but looking back… it was kind of already there. It was there way before I got married… like, back in elementary school.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I feel like I can’t really settle in my own body lately. It’s worse when I’m supposed to sit quietly and do paperwork or talk through household plans. I just get really restless.\n\nDoctor: Do you find your mind racing or having trouble focusing when you're feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does. I’ll be trying to do something at the kitchen table, and then my phone buzzes or a car goes by, or I suddenly think about dinner, and I’m just… off somewhere else before I even realize I stopped.\n\nDoctor: Is the restlessness you feel more like a physical urge to move or a feeling in your mind?\n\nPatient: More physical, I think. Like I can’t quite settle in my own body, especially if I’m supposed to sit still and deal with paperwork or household plans. I just get… uneasy.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulses when you're feeling restless, like getting up and doing something else without thinking it through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. When I’m home especially, I’ll start laundry, then suddenly I’m checking emails, then I’m reorganizing a drawer or looking at the calendar. It’s not really planned. I just kind of keep moving from one thing to the next.\n\nDoctor: Are these impulsive actions causing problems for you or those around you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I don’t really like saying that, but people at home and in the volunteer stuff I do can’t always count on me. I’ll mean to follow through, and then things just slip or get half-done. I’ve had to step back from some responsibilities because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty completing tasks because you lose interest or because you get distracted by other things?\n\nPatient: Mostly I get distracted, I think. Like if I’m at the kitchen table trying to finish something, my phone buzzes or I hear a car outside, or I suddenly think about dinner… and then I’m just off doing something else before I even realize I stopped.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this restlessness and difficulty focusing at work or in other settings outside of your home as well?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not really work, since I’m not working right now. But outside home, yes, in volunteer things. People there… and at home too… haven’t always been able to count on me, because I’ll mean to do something and then it falls through. I’ve had to step back from some responsibilities because of that.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this restlessness and difficulty focusing in social situations or when you're alone?\n\nPatient: More when I’m alone, I think… or when I’m supposed to just sit quietly and deal with paperwork or household plans. I get this feeling like I can’t really settle in my own body. In social situations, I can usually hide it a bit better.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restlessness and difficulty focusing are affecting your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. People at home, and with volunteering, they can’t always count on me the way they should. I try, but things still fall through, and I’ve had to step back from some responsibilities because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these feelings of restlessness and difficulty focusing at any time of the day, or are they worse at specific times?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure there’s a clear time of day. I haven’t really tracked it like that. It just sort of depends on what I’m trying to do, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant problems with organization or time management, such as keeping track of tasks or appointments?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I lose track of things more than I’d like. Like I’ll mean to return a call, pay a bill, or bring something with me, and then suddenly days have passed, or I’m already in the car when I remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods where your restlessness and difficulty focusing were so severe that they significantly interfered with your daily life for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s been noticeably worse since I got married and our routines kind of merged. That was about… a year and five months ago.\n\nDoctor: How old were you when you first started noticing these symptoms of restlessness and difficulty focusing?\n\nPatient: Probably elementary school. I don’t remember an exact age, but looking back… it was kind of already there. I had the messy desk, forgot permission slips, and teachers would write notes that I talked out of turn or couldn’t stay seated.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of restlessness and difficulty focusing been present for at least six months before you got married?\n\nPatient: Yes. It was there way before I got married… like, back in elementary school. I was the kid with the messy desk, forgetting permission slips, and teachers writing that I talked too much or couldn’t stay seated.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty with impulsivity in social situations, such as interrupting others or blurting out answers?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I try not to, and I really do care about being respectful, but sometimes I jump in before I’ve thought it through. Like with my husband, I’ll interrupt or finish what someone is saying because the response is just… already out before I can make myself wait.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with attention deficit hyperactivity disorder or does anyone in your family have it?\n\nPatient: I haven’t been formally diagnosed before, no. And as far as family… I’m not really sure. No one’s told me they have it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I just get really restless.\n\nDoctor: Do you find your mind racing or having trouble focusing when you're feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does. I’ll be trying to do something at the kitchen table, and then my phone buzzes or a car goes by, or I suddenly think about dinner, and I’m just… off somewhere else before I even realize I stopped.\n\nDoctor: Is the restlessness you feel more like a physical urge to move or a feeling in your mind?\n\nPatient: More physical, I think. Like I can’t quite settle in my own body, especially if I’m supposed to sit still and deal with paperwork or household plans. I just get… uneasy.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulses when you're feeling restless, like getting up and doing something else without thinking it through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. When I’m home especially, I’ll start laundry, then suddenly I’m checking emails, then I’m reorganizing a drawer or looking at the calendar. It’s not really planned. I just kind of keep moving from one thing to the next.\n\nDoctor: Are these impulsive actions causing problems for you or those around you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I don’t really like saying that, but people at home and in the volunteer stuff I do can’t always count on me. I’ll mean to follow through, and then things just slip or get half-done. I’ve had to step back from some responsibilities because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty completing tasks because you lose interest or because you get distracted by other things?\n\nPatient: Mostly I get distracted, I think. Like if I’m at the kitchen table trying to finish something, my phone buzzes or I hear a car outside, or I suddenly think about dinner… and then I’m just off doing something else before I even realize I stopped.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this restlessness and difficulty focusing at work or in other settings outside of your home as well?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not really work, since I’m not working right now. I’ve had to step back from some responsibilities because of that.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this restlessness and difficulty focusing in social situations or when you're alone?\n\nPatient: More when I’m alone, I think… or when I’m supposed to just sit quietly and deal with paperwork or household plans. I get this feeling like I can’t really settle in my own body. I lose track of things more than I’d like. It’s been noticeably worse since I got married and our routines kind of merged. It’s worse when I’m supposed to sit quietly and do paperwork or talk through household plans. I just get really restless.\n\nDoctor: Do you find your mind racing or having trouble focusing when you're feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does. I’ll be trying to do something at the kitchen table, and then my phone buzzes or a car goes by, or I suddenly think about dinner, and I’m just… off somewhere else before I even realize I stopped.\n\nDoctor: Is the restlessness you feel more like a physical urge to move or a feeling in your mind?\n\nPatient: More physical, I think. Like I can’t quite settle in my own body, especially if I’m supposed to sit still and deal with paperwork or household plans. I just get… uneasy.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulses when you're feeling restless, like getting up and doing something else without thinking it through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. When I’m home especially, I’ll start laundry, then suddenly I’m checking emails, then I’m reorganizing a drawer or looking at the calendar. It’s not really planned. I just kind of keep moving from one thing to the next.\n\nDoctor: Are these impulsive actions causing problems for you or those around you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I don’t really like saying that, but people at home and in the volunteer stuff I do can’t always count on me. I’ll mean to follow through, and then things just slip or get half-done. I’ve had to step back from some responsibilities because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty completing tasks because you lose interest or because you get distracted by other things?\n\nPatient: Mostly I get distracted, I think. Like if I’m at the kitchen table trying to finish something, my phone buzzes or I hear a car outside, or I suddenly think about dinner… and then I’m just off doing something else before I even realize I stopped.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this restlessness and difficulty focusing at work or in other settings outside of your home as well?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not really work, since I’m not working right now. But outside home, yes, in volunteer things. People there… and at home too… haven’t always been able to count on me, because I’ll mean to do something and then it falls through. I’ve had to step back from some responsibilities because of that.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this restlessness and difficulty focusing in social situations or when you're alone?\n\nPatient: More when I’m alone, I think… or when I’m supposed to just sit quietly and deal with paperwork or household plans. I get this feeling like I can’t really settle in my own body. In social situations, I can usually hide it a bit better.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restlessness and difficulty focusing are affecting your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. People at home, and with volunteering, they can’t always count on me the way they should. I try, but things still fall through, and I’ve had to step back from some responsibilities because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these feelings of restlessness and difficulty focusing at any time of the day, or are they worse at specific times?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure there’s a clear time of day. I haven’t really tracked it like that. It just sort of depends on what I’m trying to do, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant problems with organization or time management, such as keeping track of tasks or appointments?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I lose track of things more than I’d like. Like I’ll mean to return a call, pay a bill, or bring something with me, and then suddenly days have passed, or I’m already in the car when I remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods where your restlessness and difficulty focusing were so severe that they significantly interfered with your daily life for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s been noticeably worse since I got married and our routines kind of merged. That was about… a year and five months ago.\n\nDoctor: How old were you when you first started noticing these symptoms of restlessness and difficulty focusing?\n\nPatient: Probably elementary school. I don’t remember an exact age, but looking back… it was kind of already there. I had the messy desk, forgot permission slips, and teachers would write notes that I talked out of turn or couldn’t stay seated.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of restlessness and difficulty focusing been present for at least six months before you got married?\n\nPatient: Yes. It was there way before I got married… like, back in elementary school. I was the kid with the messy desk, forgetting permission slips, and teachers writing that I talked too much or couldn’t stay seated.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty with impulsivity in social situations, such as interrupting others or blurting out answers?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I try not to, and I really do care about being respectful, but sometimes I jump in before I’ve thought it through. Like with my husband, I’ll interrupt or finish what someone is saying because the response is just… already out before I can make myself wait.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with attention deficit hyperactivity disorder or does anyone in your family have it?\n\nPatient: I haven’t been formally diagnosed before, no. And as far as family… I’m not really sure. No one’s told me they have it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty sustaining focus or making careless mistakes in tasks at work, volunteer activities, or other areas outside of home?\n\nPatient: Yes… that happens. I’m not working right now, but even with things like a planning email or trying to read a chapter, I’ll start okay and then a few minutes later realize I’m just staring at the same paragraph and none of it really went in.\n\nDoctor: Have you often been forgetful in daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes… I am. I’ll mean to return a call or pay a bill, and then somehow a few days go by. Or I’ll be on my way somewhere and suddenly remember I was supposed to bring something with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties are more with paying attention and staying focused, rather than with impulsivity or hyperactivity?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s more the attention part. Like, I can sit down with an email I need to plan out, or a chapter of a book, and I start okay, but then a few minutes later I’m just staring at the same paragraph and realize none of it really went in.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I try, but things still fall through, and I’ve had to step back from some responsibilities because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these feelings of restlessness and difficulty focusing at any time of the day, or are they worse at specific times?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure there’s a clear time of day. I haven’t really tracked it like that. It just sort of depends on what I’m trying to do, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant problems with organization or time management, such as keeping track of tasks or appointments?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I lose track of things more than I’d like. Like I’ll mean to return a call, pay a bill, or bring something with me, and then suddenly days have passed, or I’m already in the car when I remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods where your restlessness and difficulty focusing were so severe that they significantly interfered with your daily life for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s been noticeably worse since I got married and our routines kind of merged. That was about… a year and five months ago.\n\nDoctor: How old were you when you first started noticing these symptoms of restlessness and difficulty focusing?\n\nPatient: Probably elementary school. I had the messy desk, forgot permission slips, and teachers would write notes that I talked out of turn or couldn’t stay seated.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of restlessness and difficulty focusing been present for at least six months before you got married?\n\nPatient: Yes. It was there way before I got married… like, back in elementary school. I was the kid with the messy desk, forgetting permission slips, and teachers writing that I talked too much or couldn’t stay seated.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty with impulsivity in social situations, such as interrupting others or blurting out answers?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I try not to, and I really do care about being respectful, but sometimes I jump in before I’ve thought it through. Like with my husband, I’ll interrupt or finish what someone is saying because the response is just… already out before I can make myself wait.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with attention deficit hyperactivity disorder or does anyone in your family have it?\n\nPatient: I haven’t been formally diagnosed before, no. And as far as family… I’m not really sure. No one’s told me they have it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty sustaining focus or making careless mistakes in tasks at work, volunteer activities, or other areas outside of home?\n\nPatient: Yes… that happens. I’m not working right now, but even with things like a planning email or trying to read a chapter, I’ll start okay and then a few minutes later realize I’m just staring at the same paragraph and none of it really went in.\n\nDoctor: Have you often been forgetful in daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes… I am. I’ll mean to return a call or pay a bill, and then somehow a few days go by. Or I’ll be on my way somewhere and suddenly remember I was supposed to bring something with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties are more with paying attention and staying focused, rather than with impulsivity or hyperactivity?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s more the attention part.
